REIICENT. OFFICIALS. 
“ NERVOUS SUDORDINATES " AND THE” 
a “ PRESS.- 


fs tow 


“The Perak Pioneer “has the following 


interesting comments on ‘offcialdom in. the |. 


Federated Malay States:—The Resident- 
General has, we understand, issued an im- 
portant circular inlerdicting Gaverament offi- 
Cials from divulging to. outsiders and third 
parties the cootents of official correspondence 
whose publication had not been authorised, 
FE ‘agit should be and it is‘believed to 
be' the ‘outcome of sundry ‘facta brought to 
the notice of. the Residebt-General’ of: the 
alleged improper use made of a <certain. 
official’ document by a .supsrior officer of 
one of the great departments But. there. 
is serious tisk of ‘such a general circular 
being misunderstood: and too narrowly inter- 
preted. by sundry timid officals and an un- 
necesnry veil of secrecy. thrown over the 
‘proceedings of Government and of its various 
departments where they could be divulged 
without prejudice to the public interest.. As a 
fact there have been already some inatances 
where nervous subordinates have’ maintained 
absolute silence even in matlers. of rontine 
refasing’ all information’ therewn even. to 
parties concerned. This cotirse of conduct 
docs all honour to their loyalty and .prudence 
but ‘is likely at times to” cause serious in- 
convenience to ‘the public.” Everybody 
knows that the Resident Geueral is a lover of 
‘publicity and courts, public ‘criticiam of all 
measures contemplated by Gove tooA 
remarkable instance of his policy io this regard 
may ‘be fotnd in'the steps he took to have the 
{ull correspondence in connection (wilh the 
Tamil Labour Bill given due publicity in time 
to ufford ample time to.evoke public criticism, 
“Gavernment is naturally: the best judge of 
what papers may be advantageously published 
without deteriment to the public interests, ‘To 
‘engute therefore due publicity of Government | 
proceedings while at. the same time sale- 
guarding the secrecy of matters Ihat may sot 
be divulged, we would suggest to the powers- 
that-be the advisability of adopting a scheme: 
whereby both these'useful purposes would be 
-served on lines similar to those adopted by the 
Indian and Provincial, Governments. - There 
they have the benefit of the services of a 
highly paid, discreét and: responsible officer, 
‘designated. ‘Press Commissioner" whose duty 
it is to communicate to the Press all mat- 
ter. of vital interest, including the official 
contradiction, -of. false or. incorrect news 
which had obtained currency. through the 
medium of apy illinformed or p rverse journal. 
Besides the agency of the Press Commissioner 
there is another method adopted by the 
Supreme and Provincial Governments of India 
to disseminate useful - public information, 
There is in every Sectetariat a room set apart 
containing: what is a aptly styled the Editors’ 
table on which are laid all official papers each 
Government had decided to piace at disposal 
of the Press, Further the Gasefle contains. a 
list of documents«50 plsced at’ its disposal 


| during the previous week. . This is an excellent 


plain, we think, meeting the growing public 
desire for aulhentic.information affecting the 
general welfare of the various communities and 
industries. “It mi ht be ‘adopted with great 
public advantage in the F, M.S. with necessary 
changes and modifications to suit local con- 
ditions. .We would advocate this‘ plan, the 
more especially that we in the F, M.S. unlike 
those in the Straits Settlements have no Legis- | 
ative Council on whose table are pl 

documents to which the Press hasve free access,

_'| THREE SUSPECTS ARRESTED. 
* Three Chinamen were arrested at West Point 
“yesterday afternoon (28th inst,) on suspicion of 
cing implicated inthe French Stre.t murder, an 
account of which appeared in our Inst evening’s 
issue. Immediately the deceased uns identified 
as Lam Choi, a ca-penter, employed at 258, 
Des Vocux Road, Sergeant’ Gordon and .a 


| Chinese, detective picked up a clue, which 


resulted in the capture of three meo, alleged'to 
be members of the Triad Society, io a Chinese 
Club, at 300, Dea Vocux Road West. 

So far the police’ are'in no way-ioclined to. 
be communicative in the’ matter, It is no 
secret, however, that a quarrel between two 
‘Trind Society clubs, known as the East and 
West Point clubs, was responsible, for the 
morder. * > 4 : tas = 

It leaked out to-day that the men in custody 


exhibit marks and bruises on their bodies— | 


a fact which goes to show that: the trio had 
been in a fight. One man io particular had 
a nasty cut on bis -finger,-which had tobe 
-dressed. From this 
| fied that the suspects took part in the engage- 
ment, which ended in the death of Lam Choi. 
Several arrests nte expected shortly.

Ata meeting of the Court of the Hongkong 
“»: Gollege' of Medicine ‘held jo tho: Legislative, 
.-,CoaacilChamber on the rdinat.,theDiplomaaf 
“the College was preseated by.the Rector, Hap, 
‘Mr. F, H. May, C.1G., to two. stodents, Kwan 
_Klog Hung. and Li Ho Ching, who have com- 
“pleted the curriculum of five years’ study and 
+ passed all the professional examinations, 
| | Mp, Li do Ching, who has seen appoint 
. to be’ Government. Medical Officer at ‘rai Fo, 
_ was also presented by the Rector witha cheque 
for $150, the amount of the Blake Scholarship, 
awarded after a competitive examination of 
the subjects of clinical surgery, clinical medi- 
. cine ard disea 1 the eye, held at the’ Gov- 
ernment Civil Hospital and the Ho Miu Ling 
* Hospital. é end : . 
--~: The College-of Medicine is making ‘an im- 
rtant forward movement at the present time. 
__ Towards the end of Jast year there came to it 
., the beginnings of an endowment, in the form 
. Of a beguest of property valued at,; 310,000, 
under the will of the late Mr, Tang Chuk Kai, 
and. this has been speedily followed by an 
offer of College buildings. ie 
, For twenty years the College has been in ex: 
« istence, quietly aod ‘continuously carrying: on 
the work it marked out for itsell in 1887. “Fhere 
"has been no Inck of students; teachers, each 
‘taking a subject for whith by previous training 
and experience ho is specially fitted, have beeo 
at all times forthcoming ; an, organization has 
existed, modelled on that of English Medical: 
Schocts'; but thus (ar the College has posseased 
no permanent local habitation, and there has: 
been no regularly, paid ‘stuff devoting itsell 
© entirely to the training of the students, i 
At length, however, College buildings ‘are 
‘pboutto be erecied. The Government two years 
ago reserved on the Tai Ping Shan esumed 
area a site in evcry way suitable: for the! pur- 
| poses of a Medical College, convenient of 


"access for Lecturers, and centrally placed‘ in 


relation to the Government Civil Hospital, 
Alice Memorial Hospital, Nethersole Howpitt 
Ho Mit Ling Hospital, ‘tung Wa Hospital, the 
Public Mortuary and the Bacteriological: La- 
boratory, tobe granted to the Court free of 
charge; nod a generous, Chinese gentleman, 
» Mr, Ng Li Hing, an o}d resident of tuts Colony, 
has now offered to spend $50,020 in the erection 
of College buildings on the site thus held io 
er has’ been’ accepted, and 


has done its utmost by makes \ 
: ‘good |its somewhat anomalous circumstances, 
It has had its headquarters in the alice 
\ Memorial: Hospital,-and the. affiliated: Nether- 
‘sole and Ho Miu Ling Hospitals have also 
“been open td students for, purposes of clinical 
instruction ; but, ib addition, the College has 


* : boreowed accommodation for special purposes 


atl-over the-citysx—During-this.sumnmer_session,_ 
for example, the Surgery lectures, have been 
delivered ‘at’ the Government Civil Hospital, 


‘Biology has been taught at Queen's College, 


Chemistry and Physics in the Queen's College 
Laboratory,. Public Health in the Royal 
Sanitary Institute's lecture hall, Pathology and 
Bacteriology in the Public Mortuary and the 
Bacteriological Institute; and the Tuog Wa, 
. Hospital has also-been made use. of in tutorial 


classes for the clinica! material it affords. ‘The }- 


Lecturers of the College are thus, it, mast be 
‘apparent, doing their best, under, the great 
disadvantages that exist, to make their teaching 
as thorough and as practical as possible: =” 
During the twenty years that have elapsed 
since the College was inaugurated, exactly 100 
students have been-enrolled; of these 31 have 
qualified, and most of the Licentianes already 
. qualified have settled in the Colony itself, All 
the Dispensaries established by the Chinese 
. themselves in various part of Victoria,in Yau- 
mati, Hunghom and old Kowloon city, for the 
* express purpose, of assisting the Government 
to enforce its sanitary laws, are manned by 
Licentiates of this-College.” Other Licentiates 
ate in direct Government service'in the New 
’ Territory and on the Railway works; some are 
resident’ surgeons in the’ lospituls for the 
Chinese ; and several are practising. their pro- 
“fession privately among their own people in 
vatious parts of the Colony, and in this capa- 
"city gaining access.to the humes and the 
confidence of {he Chinese, rich and poor alike. 
‘These men are exerting a wide; and a widen- 
ing, influence towards the breaking down of 
Chinese prejudices and Chinese obstruction to 
that better. sanitation in which, it is recognised 
on alt hands, lie-a happier future and. a yel 
_ greater commercial prosperity for this Colony. | 
We understand that it isin view to make a 
vigoroiis effort early next year to raise by pub- 
~ lic.subscription an Endowment Fund to enable’ 
the Court to engage the services of a staT-of 
specially qualified Jecturers for the more dis+ 
tinctively scientific subjects, and to tender” 
. more effective by the provision of adequate 
teaching appliances the~work of the present 
honorary staff of lecturers, who will continue 
their services to the institution

A PECULIAR ACCIDENT. 


ne 
CHINAMAN FALLS-INTO CAULDRON OF 
‘BOILING SUGAR, * 


‘A most-unique experience was that which 
+” occurred to a, young-Chinese cauldron attend: 
‘antin-a sugar. boiling establishment. at 2 Sai 
‘Woo Lane, fast Sunday. Standing ona platform, 

_ over a cauldron of boiling sugar, the lad was 
busy stirring the contents. He discovered 

_ aftera while that the sugar was congealing 
and, obtaining a bucket of water he was in the 
act of pouring it into the inside edges of the 


cauldron when he fell headlong into the | 


boiling sugar, which was bubbling up to 
a depth of four feet. The fokis standing 
nearby immediately: pulled the unfortunate 
Doy-out, of the cauldron and had him re-. 
moved to hospital, where every care ‘was 
‘shown him.”'On ‘account of. the: © peculiar 
setae. ot hie injuries he is not expected to 
ive, :

: >. Wuchow, 24th August, 1907, 

A coupte of outbreaks of fire oceurre here on 
the 22nd instant, but luckily the damages wore 
not serious ip, elther case. . The first oulbreak 
toak placo at noon in a Chinese house at the 
foot ofthe Consular hill, A'mob soon gathered 
with buckets of water and though their atlempts 
were ineffectiat ns regards. saving the buming 
house they succeeded ‘in preventing the fire 
spreading to: the. adjoining houses, The 
econd fire broke ‘out in a cargo boat moored 
longaide Jardine’s pontaon aud was the result 
of careless burning of joss papor. Before the 

fire could get a good:hold it.was promptly put 
out ‘by :the aid of the’caigo boat's crews lying 
alongside. Had this fire spread a serious con- 
Mlagration might have retulted. : Loi” 
VU S op FATAL FIGHTS 

* The fatat affray onthe foreshore, reported in. 
your columns of the 21st August bas resulted 
in the’ death of ‘the. other combatant. The 
Police, in spite of a serious protest from the 
native.merchants, have refused to take this case 
up. Both victims have beeo placed in coffins 
on the foreshore and have‘ remuined there for 
figarly a week:without burial, ‘ow long they 
are'to ba ‘thus treated remaingto be geen, but 
great indiguation prevails amongst the Chi- 
nese here, at this state of affairs. The ‘Police 
state itis mone of their, busisess and, the City 
Magistrate equally disclaims any responsibility, 
It in probable thatthe. two unfortunate victims 
‘will be buried by public native subscription 
and that. complaint: will be’ made to some 
higher authority to sed. that somebody gets a 
“wigging” over this sad affair. aS 

_._ CHARTERED JUNK TROUBLE. 

It is, reported that’ the master of-one of the 
chartered junks plying between here and-Nan- 
hing has bee arrested io Nanning. From 
what can be gathered. it appears that this junk 
master, whose junk is chartered by a forergo’ 
firm here, on-arcival:at Kwai Usien, was board: 
ed by three men in a sampan who demanded to 
see the junk's papers. Thejank captain relused 
to show his. papers until he was satisfied that 
these men hada righttoses them. The three 
men in question stated (hey were Likin officials, 
but had no uniform, flag or other authority and 
on being told that the junk was chartered toa 
foreign hony and under’a foreign flag, they 
became nasty and a row ‘ensued. - Eventually 
the three men. went ashore, On arrival at 
Nanning the junk master was, arrested on 
a complaint trom the Kwel Hsien Likin 
officinis; who alleged that one’ of . their 
meo was thrown overboard from this junk and 
drowned. this the junk people deny. An 
inquisy is now being held. in Nanning into 
this matter, but it is commonly said. that the 
harge is a trumped up one by the Likin, offi- 

jals. This incident is of great interest to the 

shipping firms here as it shows the -Likin offi: 
cials don't look favourably upon the" chartered, 
juak system.” Of course a chartered iynk pays 

no Likin fees but is responsible for maritime 

Customs duty, etc, and this is. probably the 

kernel of the row. The matter will no: doubt 
be definitely settled ina way that a. repetiti 
of the above incident will not” be likely to ore

MRADE MARKS IN CHINA. 

The following is a free translation of a Pro- 
clamation recently issued by bis Honour Tao: 
tai Jui Cheng on the subject of British Trade 
Mar’ : ; : : 
1, Tul Cheng, the’ Taotai of" Shanghai, etc, 
.etc., do hereby issue this proclamation for the 
information of the public that H.B.M. Consul- 
General, Sir P: L. Warren, has written me to 
the effect that he has repeatedly received com- 
pla‘nts. from British merchants in regard to 
Chinese. manufacturing and setailing certain 
qualities of English-made goods, purporting 
same to be manufactured by those factories, 
for which they (the British merchants): are 
the sole agents, ‘This isa great detriment to 
the British interests, The quality of the goods 
which are imitated according to Englislr pat-- 
terns-is very poor and of great difference, 
although the packing, colour, per and chop: 
are quite similar to those of, English make. 
Therefore when the Chinese is charged with 
copying patterns he'generally denies the charge 
by pointing dut the slight difference of patterns: 
between the two. kinds of yoods. This is a 
very intolerable business competition in the 
market, Recently such malpractice is daily 
increasing, and, therefore, litigation on’ this 
question will keep on increasing also. As 
such, would more. or. less strain the com- 
mercial relaiion of the two countries, he, 
requests me to prohibit further copying of 
patterns by ‘Chinese people: ‘the . Chinese 
Chamber of Commerce, upon being noti-- 
fied of this-malter, bas replied that’ such 
prohibition is the right. step taken , for cam- 
mercial protection, and asks ta be informed 
of the different patlerns which are most fra- 

ently. being copied ia order to co-operate in, 
the prohibition, In compliance with my re- 
quest the British Consul-General has furnish: 
ed me witha list of tt 
factured by the. British-American Tobacco 
Co., Ld., Depét., and also g list of the soaps, 
for which Messrs A. R. Burkill and Sons 
are the sole ngents; these two articles are 
more often manufactured by the native accord: | 
ing to their’ patterns.. The Consul-General 
says that he has on several nccasions called 
the attention of-the Viceroy_of Nanking to this 
matter, and, moreover, he requests me to, issuc 
this proclamation to be posted everywhere to 
prohibit further imitation of English-made 
goods, The Chhmber of Commerce havin3 
been asked to observe this' order, ! hereby 
warn the general public that henceforth such 
malpractice on the part of ovr people should 
once and for all be stopped. Anyone found to 
be violating this law will be punished without 
leniency,—¥. C. D. News, ‘

THE GREAT TOK YO EXHIBITION. 
1 ——> ae ts 
“SUGGESTED "EDUCATIONAL FEATURES 


Considerable public -attention has been 
drawo to the great Exhibition to be held ia 
Tokyo in 1912, by the appointment -of the ! 
sident and other, officiats. ‘The Nippon re- 
marks that the coming Exhibition is not purely 
a world's exhibition in its, nature, but the Go- 
vernment intends to make ita World’s Fair so 
far as is practicable, and it is necessary for the 
Government and people to combine and push | 
on the preparation for that end. ee 

As the first step, contioues the Tokyo journal 
it is imperative to define the general police ¢ 
the scheme. The main object of an exhibition 
is too obvious to be set forth here, but it is im- 
portant to -add special arrangements to the 


ordinary details of an éxhibition, in considera | 


tion ‘of the requirements of time Sod circum- 
stances, and thus make it the more altractive. 
Ourcontemporary warmly endorses the word” 
of Baron Kaneko that the coming Exhibition 
is an enterprise aiming at the attraction of for- 
eign visitors, and so all. must unite in exercis= 
ing their energies to attract foreigners, and 
show the world that the Japanese people have 
the effective power of a first-class nation: in 
matters of industry and commerce as well as 
in military affairs, The Aipfon then points 
out that it is necessary to inspire the Japanese 
with fall knowledge of the new position of, 
apan as a first-class country, and thus give an. 
incentive for the future activity of Japan in- 
the commerce of the world. The posi- 
‘Vion “acquired by Japan as the result. of 
the Japan-China abd’ the Japan-Russia’ 
wars is new not only to foreigners, but 
to the Japanese themselves. The Japanese 
have not yet realised their own position, just 
as most foreigners are ignorant of thiogs 
Japanese, and it ‘is to be hoped, therefore, that. 
special. arrangements will be made to make 
Japanese and foreigners alike realise Japan's 
pew positian, For thia pares the Tokio 
journal recommends that the hibition should 
be mads to show the Japanesh how extensive 
is the territory newly acquired, held usder a 
protectorate, occupied, or within Japan's sphere 
of influence, It should also be showo how 
productive are these lands, and in what con- 
dition are they, so that the Japanese will realise. 
the nature and extent of the new Empire, and 


be made to feel themselves actually in thesa, 


now-territorics. . 

It is. a well-known 
that the Japanete have a very fimited kaow 
ledge . of these. new territories. Jt is also 
necessary to introduce to them Siberit, the, 
Amur district, French Indo-China, Siam, 
British India, aud the South Sea' Islands, and. 
for this purpose these countries should be 


invited to send exhibits, A. special building | 


for each of these countries shquid be erected, 


where -they would’ probably’ not.sefise_to 
“Japan

“The Singajore Free Preas devotes the follow:

servants and their proposed registration — 

. [tis impossible to note the nature of the 
cases that come. up before the police courts: 
without remarkiog that 9 cansiderable propor- 
tion of these refer to the dishbnesty of domestic 
servants. A considerable proportion, we say, 
when it emembered thatthe domestic ser- 
vant class does not in ‘the least suffer.from un- 
employment, is os a iule better paid.than it 
should be, and cannot. therelore be said to. be 
‘impelled towards theft from, the. pressure of 
want, Again it is difficult to “arrive “at any 
estimate of the cases of dishonesty Which do 
occur but -remain undetected, and even, if 


-buspected to lie at the door ofn particular ser- | : 


vant, do not result in complaint to the police, 
much less prosecution. Itis a matter of general 
opinion here that domestic servants in the 
Straits are templed into dishonesty by the. 
opportunities that are inevitable, but‘ even: 
more by. the immuniy that they enjoy from 
the absence of any check such os a proper 
system of registration would provide,

Oo Tuesday (Aug. 13) his Excellency Luke

States, carrying. with. him the goodwill and 
esteem tiot only of his own nationals, but of

other foreigners resident in the country.

AN ASSASSINATION IN TOKYO, 


_ The Japan Chronicle of 16'h in : 
Japanese subject named Mayeda Seiji, suspect-, 
ed of rain a Russian sky, was assassinated in 
broad daylight in a’ Tokyo street on Wednes:; 
day afternoos. The father of the unfortunate; 
man is said to be a Russian now deceased, and 
Mayeda was born in: Tokyo by the Russian’s 
Japaneso wife. He had been living in Viadi- 
vostok and during the war acted as interpreter .’’ 
tothe Russian army, He assived' in Tokyo — 
recsatly, which: aroused’a ‘great deal of public 2 
attention... . Mow ft My 
-- Op Wednesday Mayeda was talkiog with 


mao named Imamura Seitaro, an-employé of 


‘the Japanese Sewing Machine Company. in, 
the street close to Shiba park, when the latter, 
suddenly drew his sword-stick and thrast ihe: « 
blade foto the other man’s right hand. The, 
supposed spy ran off, followed by hi Naot, - 
who overtook. Mayeda: in front of the Shiba. 
police station. There he again stabbed him 
with the sword, and this. praved fatal, for the

MARINE HAWKERS PLIGHT. 
HRIBING A LUKONG. 


With his hand resting on the shoulder of his: 
youngest son, his wife standing close by, look- 
ing a picture of misery, Li Sze,an aged marine 
hawker, wept as if his heart would break in the 
waiting room of ‘the Magistracy, last Tuesday. 
morning, atthe thought of leaving them (or some 
months,. The scene was a most pathetic one. 
The old man had committed .a breach of the 
law and he had to pay for it. 

The charge’ under which he was convicled 
was that of‘unlawful possession and offering a 
bribe to a policeman. On Monday afternoon 
while out purchasing old stores Li Sze was 
-Sopped by. Lukong 225 in Connaught Road 
Central, who proceeded ‘to search his baskets, 
The-policeman found under a mass of rubbish 
| four. slabs of zinc, which he suspected to have 
been stolen property: Obtaining no satisfac- 


tory explanation from the marine hawker he. 


placed him under arrest. .On the -way to the 
siation.the prisoner handed the lukong $1.20 
“to buy tea.” At the Central Police Station, 
Inspector Ritchie, who was on charge-room 
duty, ordered the marine hawker's house to be 
searched. This resulted in the finding of a 
steam gauge, a quantity of rubber packing,.a 
sleam whistle, two spoons, one of which be- 
longed to the Hongkong Club, and a razor. 
When. he was araigned before Mr, F. A. 
Hazeland on Tuesday morning, the marine 
hawkey stated that he purchased the godds on 
his daily rounds, "He could not produce’ the 
persons who sold them to him. -He did not 
deny bribing the policeman, oe see 
His Worship fined him $100, with the alter- 
native of-three months’ imprisonment, ‘That 
sum could not be raised and he went to gaol, 


‘| his wife and son accompanying him as far as

THE DISADVANTAGES OF PROTECTICN... 


Ia dealing with’ the above “important and 


“joteresting” question, the Jijf once again 


criticises the hhne of policy followed by ‘the 
japanese. Government in drawing up the 
Customs Tariff, -as.tending to hamper the 
development of co-operation between Japanese 
and foreigners in developing ‘industries in 
Japan, which practice will otherwise tend to 
grow fapidly. vt : 

‘The Tokyo journal points‘ out that: foreign 
capitalists who aro. inclined to invest their 
money in. Japanese Bonds will probably not 
belong content with the fixed rate of return 
for their money as the mere holders of Bonds. 
It is only natural that they will be tempted to 
take industrial business in Japan uoder their 
own. control asd so get. better returns, 
The benefit accraing to Japan from such 

ngs as not 
only does it bring in an abundant supply of 


money and soimprove industrial works but it 


also seaults ip more skilled methods and better 
results.. The issue of foreign Bonds is not.the 
only means available for introducing foreigh 
money, and moreover the amount of moncy 
which can be introduced ‘by this means is 
limited. The unsatisfactory reaut of the issue 
of South Manchuria- RailWhy debentures 
lately in Londah may. be due to'the money 
stringency in the London market, but issue. of 
the lage amount of the war. Bonds, followed 


-by the Tokyo“ municipal_and_railway_Bonds,. 
‘which has flooded the London market with 


Japanese stock, is largely responsible for failure. 
If tisnecegsary to introduce foreign money, 
contends the Tokyo journal, it is imperative to 
adopt some other means, ; aa ej 

The coalition of Japanese and foreigners in 
undertaking industrial business in this country 
is a means which will be found commendable. 
When foreigners acquire a full knowledge of 
Japanese matters by experience in co-operation 
with Japanese, and come to undertake business 
themselves foreign money will flow-into the 
country without limit, and Japanese business- 
men are. secommended by the //j? to. avail 
themselvesol this means for introducing foreign 


‘noney, instead of by meaus of the issus of 


Bonds, ee 
| It-isto be regreited, our ‘contemporary. pro- 


ceeds, that the ‘tine.of policy fol’owed by the 


Goverament in commerce a jd industry has a 
tendency to hamper the introduction of foreign 
money. For instance, the tariff policy is framed 


| for the protection of agriculture. Duties are 


imposed on cereals and other food-stuffs, seeds, 
and.on articles which are produced in‘ Japan. 
This policy not only impedes.the development 
of industry, but also’hinders the inflow of for- 
cign money to be invested in’ industrial enter~ 
prises, — The fiji Points out that the object -of 
the foreig..er coming to this country with mo- 
Ney to set up in industrial work isnot to su 
ply goods to Japan only but alsé to the nei 
bourng countries, and thus to save traub!e 
expense in bringing. goods out from bh 
distant homeland, The imposition of duties 
on raw materials and food-stuffs, aa practised. 
in’ this country of late, will heavily increase, 
producing expences, and the foreign capitalist 
Intending to ‘promote industrial: works ‘in 
Japan will consequently be discouraged. . The 
increased. expenses of production must be 
borne by the manufacturers, who- have 
thus to lose a share of this profit. So far as 
the present Customs policy of the Government 


.is maintained, itis impossible to hope for in- 


dustrial development in Japan. The protective 


‘policy will not only prevent foreign capitalists 


from coming to Japan with. money to start 
industrial works, but wll 1 ad Japanese capi 
talists to seek investment for their. money in 
foreign lands where cheaper food and watorial 
can be obiained. The resale will be that 
Japanese capital will be driven from this coun- 
try, and the flow.of foreign money will be 


checked. An .opition prevails that the pra- |’ 
tective policy will encourage foreign manufac- |- 
turers to .statt their works in Japan .for|. 


the purpose. of enjoying the alleged benefits 
of protection, 
of schemes for. starting icdustrial works 
by Japanese and ‘foreigners in co-operation and 
this is ascribed by some to the anticipation of 
the revision of the treaties due in a few years, 
which will entail a further increase in the Cus- 
toms | Tariff.

official’ barriers between the two classes 
of the people. The decree, issued on August’ 
gin the name of the Empress. Dowager, ctin- 
manding officials both in Peking and in. the 
provinces to report on tle present, position be- 
-tween Manchus and Chinese and to make sug- 

estions for the removal of thé evils of racial 
Jealousy, reflects true anxiety -to do away with 
causes’ of offence in spite of the pretentious 
platitudes with whieh it opens. - On the other, 


‘| hand the attitude of genuine-reformers-on-the-|}- 
h same subject indicates that in. their opinion the- 
‘old controversy between Chinese ahd Manchus 


has a distinct bearing on the Somewhat parlous; 
condition ‘of China to-day, - The necessity of 
ab lishing the favoured ‘position of Munchus 
officials and others, lias been urged successive- 
ly by she Viceroys Yuan Shih-kai and Chang 
Chih-tuag, by the. Govérnar of Soochowand by

Canten in’ succession to H.E. Tsén Chun- 
hsuen; and in the proposal to temodel. the 
Tartar garrisons throughout the . Empire, 
which are a stumbling-block to the good rela- 
tions between the two sections of the people 
whereever they ate established. A Censor has 
found yet anotter cause‘ for bitter feeling in 
the .fact, that the period of mourning for 
parents is fixed at one hundred days for 
Manchus, but at three years for Chinese: 
The result of this law is to bar the latter from 
holding office, for a period Iwelve times “as 
long as ins more favoured Manchu colleague 


hin the event of a parent's death, 


All such atiacks ‘on the privileged position 
of the Manchus aré stepsin the right direction, 
but as yet there is no indication of the length 
-of time required by them to achieve success.! 
Old strongholds, especially when walled, in 
with the prestige of centuries, surrender ua- 
willingly, and neither the fear, or statesman- 
ship, of the Empress-Dowager,.nor the anxiety 
for the country’s welfare ofa Yuan Shib-k’ai cr 
Chang Chih-tung has found any ready echo 
in Mancho circles as a whole. Where con- 
scious that their sun of exceptional privi- 
lege hag passed its meridian,- they: still 


. Hope to arrest the course of nature by a com- 


promise on’ non-essentials, while secretly’ re- 
solved to retain the substance of their power. 
The revived attention now being paid to the 
question of constitutional government will not 
have escaped public noti It is difficult in 
the circumstances to dis nguish the genuine 
activity of the enthusiasts in the cause of reform 
from the time-serving acquiescence of its most 
rabid opponents. Dut-if we accept the position 
of this: movement as reports from the Capital 
represent it, we fiod that already a sharp fine 
of cleavage between Manchus and Chinese 
has made itself felt. The Jatter, in the 
character of reformers, affect to see no per- 
manent. solution of present difficulties in 
any remedy less comprehensive than self-co- 
vernment on a wide popular basis. On the 
other hand the Manchu, even where prepared 
to admit the advisabilily* of‘ constitstional 
government, would restrict ifs scope-to the 
narrowest limits, While the champions af the 
two causes are fighting their academic batiles 
in Peking, the ultimate destiny of the country 
is being evolved in the cause taken by the 
development of China's millions, Whether 
that destiny will be attained by peaceful means 
or through’ the instrumentality of internecine 
struggle, it is too early.to say; but it is not 
difficult to decide which has hitherto bees the 
chosen method of history. C. 2. News,.

A STRANGE INCIDENT. 


{From Our Own Correspondent.) .. 
£9 ..’* Canton, 23rd August. 
On the evening of the zist inst.,'a foreigner 
accampanied by his boy paid a visit to the Po 
Wah theatre in the eastern section of the new 
bund. About midnight, the whole theatre was 
froused by an alarm raised, by the foreigner 
that he bad been relieved of a $2000 cheque 
He laid the blame on his 
boy, complaining that he was careless in look- 
ing after him, and furthermore that he is alleg- 
ed to have been in ‘league ‘with ‘some ‘other 
rascalsin committing the theft. The:foreigner 
at once seized the boy and it remains to be seen 
what will bs the outcoma of the matter.

At 11 o'clock yesterday a fire broke out ing 
matting shop at Yau Lan Mun. ‘The fite burnt 
fiercely for a time, but. owing to the timely 
assistance of the different fire brigades, the out- 
break ‘was extinguished before. much damage 
was done tothe. neighbourhood. One building 
“was completely destroyed in the conflagration. 
On the same-day at 4. p.m. another outbreak 
of fire took place ina building adjoining the 
Kwok See Pao office, but the flame was sup- 
‘pressed at once, so no serious-damage was 
done, ua ot oe :

" QUESTION OF PRIVATE RICKSHAS AT - 
WEST POINT. ‘ . 

"Police Sergeant Gordon, of. No. 7 Police’ 
Station, brought a-tesi case before Mr. Fy. A. 
Hazeland. at the Police Court, last Thursday, 
for settlement.. The case was that in which a 


-ricksha coolie wascharged with “plying”a private 


vehicle for hire witheut a licence. A practice 


-exista among the mistresses of certain houses: 


of ill-fame at Shek-tong-teui to keep 9 number’ 
of private rickshas.on the premises. ‘At night 
these vehicles are -bired out to the singing girls 
of the house, who pay the mistress for the use 
of the vehicle, thereby, the police ‘allege, de- 
fraiding the publicricksha coolie. And it was 
with this object in view that the police brought 
check to the 


” His Worship (to accus )—Have you a per- 
mit to ply a ricksha ?—It is a private ricksha, 
Sergeant Gordon said that at midnight on 


Wednesday he saw accused pulling a private |- 


ricksba along Queen’s Road West. He hada 
fare. The sergeant siopped him and asked for 
the owner of the vehicle. Accused took him to 
a certain diso'derly house lower dows the road. 


Arriving there the fire gotout, Asked if the 


fare wat his employer accused replied in the 
negative, a : 

His Worship—Does 
live in that house? . - nh 

Witness—-Yes, your, Worship.. But the yick- 
sha does not belong toher. Itis a well-ksown 
practice among the women of the disorderly 
houses at West Point to purchase as many as 
twenty and thir y rickshas for the use of their 


the woman—the fare— 


houses, These rickshas are let out to the in-: 


mites, who pay. the mistress each time a 
vehicle is used, while” the mistress pay the 
coolie. : ; : 
His Worship—This point has been raised 
before, and I must look into. the matter. ' 
Witness—Many private rickshas from the 
city are hired to these houses, your Worship. 
. His Worship ~'s that so? Can you. prove it? 
Witness— here is not aricksha to be seen 
during the daytime, but at night there are as 
many as 200 running to and from the eating 
houses and the disorderly houses. '” 
The defendant was called to the stand, and 


he‘ swore that he was engaged to drive the 
‘people of the house; A . 


his Worship—Do ‘you-drive people other 
than those from your ‘mistress’s house ?—No. 
His Worship held that there:-was no. breach 
of the law committed. The prosecution did 
not prove that accused “plied” for hire, neither 
did they prove that an outsider bad engaged 
accused's ricksha, and unless they could prove 
that he could do nothing. The accused. was: 
discharged. anes st :

iZHE COMET, 

It is.not everybody wha cares to get up at 4 
am. or so, But those who do, and if the 
morning. be clear and starlit, have a good 
opportunity of, seeing the new visitant to the 
rcalnis of this solar syftem. Perhaps the best 
Way for the Inexperienced observer is to look 
out for the rise over the eastern horizon of 


‘Orion, whose three stars in a straight line 


take that the most e: recognised of gll the 
constellations. After Orion is well above the 
horizon the eye shoutd travel ab wut thirty-five 


| degrees to the north of east,.the left hand, 


when the tail of the comet may be descrie 
rising above. the, horizon, Theo finally the 
nucleus, a bright stellar body, will-appear, If 


| the observer is on the sea front the comet will 


ise somewhat to the«north of Johore Hill, 
_in the direction of Tampenis. As it is approach: 
ing the ‘sun it. will rise‘ later each morning 
uotil it ‘cannot be made out in the grow- 
ing brightness of the sunrise. The comet will 
be at its brightest on Wednesday. morning 
about five o'clock, and will not be seen’ from 
‘any point where trees obscure- the easter, 
horizon, A. homo paper gives a réference to 
the comel, which of course is nothing like the 
giant: of 1858 and 1°61. For areal big thing 
in Comets, we shall have to wait thres years 
more, . There’ may bo a lew very old gentle: 
mep and ladies who may, have. seen Halley's. 
comet in 1835. But few of these will survive 
to renew ‘their acquaintance with the same 
comet in 1910, when it is due to reappear. “And 
the great majority cf those now living had 
better take a good look at Halley's. Comet 
when it does turn up, because they will not 
have another. chance till 1895, by which time 
the majority of ua will be'taking no interest 
things sublunary.” This is th: allusion to 


are that.on June 9. 
American astronomer detected this comet as it 
was coming ‘towards the earth, arid noted it ag 
visible in a.small telescope. The announce 
Sient once having been made,. other asi 
nomers koowing where to look, made measures 
of the position of the newcomer,-s2 that suffi- 
cient information has been accumulated, from 
which its future path,can be: predicted. It 
would be-out‘of place to write here in the 
technical language of astronomy, but the course 
of things may be mentally pictured by suppor 
ing a hansom cab, representing the coming from 


infinite space down Piccadily, passing through || 


the circus tound the south ‘and east side of 
the refuge, and leaving ‘it by Shaftesbury- 
avente; whilst we, representiog the “earth, 
move mure leisurely in a circle round the 
allegorical statue, which for the. moment is 
to be taken as the equivalent.of the'sun, On. 
June 9, when the first ghservation was inade, 
the earth in this plan was somewhere apposite 
the Quadrant and the observer’ caught the 
‘comet coming ‘into the circus. on the south: 
pavement of Piccadilly, By Sept. 3, when we 
shall have made scarcely a quarter of the 


circuit from our position on June 9, the comet |” 


will have travelled more than ‘half across tho 
roadway, and looked at from the statue will be 
in the direction of Coveatry-street.. This is 
the date of perihelion position, when the comet 
is nearest the-sun, and for that reason will thea 


have its.greatest intrinsic brightness, but to us}: 


on the earth it will appear brightest on Aug. 
-21, for. at that time our nearness to the comel,, 
which ma, be inferred from the relative posl-, 
tion of the two ‘objects in the above flippant, 
‘analogy will compeusate forthe actual inferior 
lustre of the comet. a :

We do not often ‘hear of sizictly honest Chi- 
nese coolies 80 that the following story is as 
refreshing as it is true... A gentleman who was 
leaving Chinwangtao the other day to return 
to Tientsin. gave a coolie what he thought was 
20, cents for moving his luggage. ‘The cain 
was’ brought back by the man in. about five 
minutes with the remark in Chinese: “This 
is a golden pound,,} do not want it.” [t turn- 
ed out to be a sovereiya, of ‘which the genue- 
man had several in his pocket at the time, and 
he had paid the man one by mistake. 
honesty of the man was the more surprising as. 
he'had been in South Africa, and was well ac- 
quainted with the value of English money. Ho 
received a dollar as a reward. —Chint Critle. :

STHAMAKS.: a 


I bas ‘been, our melancholy duty: within “the 
past few: days to record the death of several 
matine officers and engitieers on’ boatd of 
coasting steamers from choleri, and the 
occurrence of’ these sad events once more 
gives point-to the complaints which one so} 


| frequently hears from members of the shipping. 


community to the effect that sufficient attention 
is not always. paid. on merchant - vessels; 


especially on those engaged in the coast trade, | 
tothe preparation and care of food. From all we {| 


cam here on the subject it would seem ‘that the 
ohip’s i ice. -cheal is most frequently the’ subject 
eglect._The_yalle 
‘invisiably spotlessly kept, and the food scund 
and wholesome when it goes on board}, but in 
the case of too many vessels belonging.to local 
companies 'the ice-chest is not attended to with 
anything like. the care or assiduity ‘that, should 
be devoted 1 i. [tis not scoured out free 
quently “enough, and itis seldom properly dis- 


-infecied-or sterilised-in.any- -way j-and- the result: 


is that froit, fish and: vegetables, jammed into 
the chest logéther, a8 they too often are, be 
come contaminated and. peo,le who eat 
them .cen.ract pto naine poisoning, which 
nianifests itself in the familiar, fatal form of 
cholera. An expedient so si ple of application 
that we marvel it isnot in use on all the 
st ame § engaged ‘in the local'trade has just 
been brought’ to our notice by a shipping friend, 
who i forms us that il is in use on a ‘good. 
many of Messrs, Butterfield & Swire's. 
vessels, which - shave: 10 far this year en 
joyed a happy immunity from’ cholera, 
which we sincerely hope will continue to 
‘atteod them. ° She « expedient referred to 
consists io , Seonnecting some. handy’ ateam- 
Pp pes say—that which feeds the winch,—with 
the ice-chést, and at convenient intervals turn- 
ing on a full head of steam into the chest, 
which should, of course, be kept tightly 
closed, and subjected to the action of the 
scalding hot steam for a couple of. Hours, By 
this means: every corner and crevice of the 
interior of the chest ‘would be reached by 
the steam, which tis an excellent’ sterilising 
medium, and the receptacle, when it became 
cool again, which wovld be in a ‘couple. of 
hours efter the steam had been. turned of, 
‘would be {pund‘to be perfectly sweet and |: 
wholesime for the storage of food. “As we say 
it seems to be a very simple, economical acd 
effective expe ient, and there is ao ‘Teason, 
why it should not be generally adopted, 
The importance of a clean ice-chest. cannot 
be over-estimated ;. we bel.eve wa are jostified 
in saying that three-fourths of the cases of 
cholera and diarthaca which occur at sea 
during the ‘summer months are to be traced 
directly to the ice-chest ; and if the” means we 
hive suggested for purifying: this most import: 
ant article of ships! furniture should pot be 
found practicable, some other way” should be 
sought and adopted without delay.— Shanghai
